### X.

# *Conversation of the Soul.*

1. What do I see here, O Jesus <note>original: "Jefu" — the Latin vocative form of Jesus, used here in direct prayer.</note>, in this Figure; namely: the Cross, with all kinds of instruments of suffering <note>The "Instruments of Suffering" (Arma Christi) refer to the objects associated with the Passion, such as the nails, crown of thorns, and spear, which were common subjects for meditation in 17th-century devotion.</note>, with which you were once burdened and weighed down, suffering many pains outside of me, yet for the sake of my sins, and for my benefit: So also may you desire to carry the Cross and all such instruments internally within me.

2. And the same Love, which moved you not only to take up such a Cross and instruments of suffering, but also to willingly carry and endure them to the end, may that same Love also move you to willingly take up and endure this your Cross in me, so that I may come into the fellowship of your suffering with you.

3. Let, O good Jesus, this your Cross be my soul’s only medicine, my only refuge in all temptations <note>original: "aanvechtingen" — this term refers to spiritual trials, doubts, or "assaults" by the devil that test a believer's faith.</note>, my protection against the Enemy, and my only comfort in life and death.

4. And you, my soul, concern yourself with this night and day, so that you [may receive] Jesus with his Cross

<keywords>Cross, fellowship of suffering, spiritual medicine, temptations, inner life, Jesus, Arma Christi</keywords>

5. ### Tenth Figure.

Dearest Jesus! <note>original: "Jefu" — the Latin vocative form of Jesus, used here as a direct address in prayer.</note> shelter your cross,
And bring your rod of discipline <note>original: "tugtroe" (tuchtroede) — literally a "discipline rod," referring to the trials sent by God to correct and refine the believer’s soul.</note> into our house:
Let all your suffering, death, and pain,
Always be imprinted in our heart;
Then no idle honor,
No worldly lusts shall avail us anymore.
This little bundle of pure myrrh, <note>A reference to Song of Solomon 1:13: "A bundle of myrrh is my well-beloved unto me; he shall lie all night betwixt my breasts." In mystical tradition, the "myrrh" represents the bitterness of Christ’s Passion kept close to the heart.</note>
Let it be enclosed within our heart;
Thus we are free from all evil,
That passes us to and fro.

---

When the holy Cross of the Lord appears often in the heart
Of man, as does his bitter suffering,
His soul is very easily freed from vanity;
And sets aside the world’s joy and its pleasure.

---

Jesus, with your Cross and wounds,
Refresh <note>original: "Laaft" (laven) — to quench thirst or provide spiritual refreshment.</note> my soul at all times;
Let all your grief and bitter pain
Be for its refreshment.

->JUDE VS. 20.<- <note>original: "JUDÆ" — the Latin form of Jude. In many older Bibles, this citation refers to what is now verse 21.</note>

*Keep yourselves in the love of God.*

When, O dear Jesus! <note>original: "Jesu" — the Latin vocative form of Jesus, used in direct address.</note> my soul lies bowed down
In deep sadness;
Then you, Lord, place your suffering before my eyes,
And your obedience;
Then my soul must think in deep humility <note>original: "ootmoet" — a seventeenth-century term for humility or submission before God.</note>,
What do I suffer compared to that pain,
Which you suffered then, when you came to give yourself,
For our loving heart.
O dearest Son! obedient to your Father!
To bring us, who were far away,
Treading the path of sin, closer to you;
Where can another such as you be found?
Your great love cannot be measured,
It has neither limit nor end,
Oh, let it never be forgotten by us,
Whom you know in love.
Sweetest Lord, imprint your suffering, cross, and pain,
Your soul's grief and agony,
Yes, bring it all, as it pleases you, into the heart,
Made entirely pure by you:
Then your cross, your death, and bitter suffering,
Will be imprinted in my soul,
So that I can turn away from those sins
Known better to you than to myself. <note>The author suggests that God perceives the depth of human sin even more clearly than the sinner does, yet offers love regardless.</note>

### Observations on the
## X. Figure.

->Concerning the words of ISAIAH 63: 2, 3.<-

> Why is your apparel red? and your garments like one who treads in the winepress? I have trodden the winepress alone, and there was no one from the nations with me.

---

### S O N G.
->Tune: O behold, most beautiful Nymph, etc. <note>original: "Ey ſchoonſte Nimph aanſiet" — This was a popular secular melody of the 17th century. It was common practice (known as contrafactum) to set new religious lyrics to well-known worldly tunes to make them easier to learn.</note><-

->1.<-

My love, I must weep from ardent love,
->Why are you so covered in sweat,<-
And why do your clothes look like one,
->Who treads in the winepress?<-
As water-brooks stream from a mountain,
My love, my most beautiful Lord,
So that bloody purple flows down
->Even unto your hems.<-

### COUNTER-SONG.

->2.<-

Therefore, O Bride, across all my limbs,
->My garments are so red,<-
Because I have trodden the press alone,
->It is the blood that I shed:<-
Out of love, to quench your fainting heart,
My dove, my most beloved Bride!
Arise and go out of yourself, <note>The phrase "gaat u ſelven uyt" (go out of yourself) refers to the mystical practice of self-abnegation or ecstasy, where the soul leaves behind worldly concerns and ego to unite with the Divine.</note>
->I shall pour it out for you.<-

The text is structured as a "Song" and "Counter-song," representing a mystical dialogue between the Soul (the Bride) and Christ (the Groom).</summary>

### Further Reflection.

## From AMBROSE, <note>Referring to St. Ambrose of Milan (c. 340–397 AD), a prominent theologian and one of the four original Doctors of the Church.</note>

->Regarding the 10th Figure.<-

Behold here the deep humility, wonderful patience, zealous love, overflowing goodness, wondrous meekness, and constant obedience of Jesus Christ! Behold here the pain, sorrows, anxieties, struggles, and extreme sufferings of Christ for the spiritual and eternal good of the precious souls of his redeemed; Behold here the death of Christ; See how he bowed his Head and gave up his spirit.

Well, these are the particular aspects to which we must become conformed. But alas! What a wide, broad, and great difference, distinction, and inequality is there between me and him? Christ’s graces shone in his suffering; his virtues revealed themselves in his passion like so many stars in a clear winter night. But how weak and dark are the faint graces in my own Soul? Christ suffered so much for me in his passion, more than I can know; through his unknown sorrow <note>original: "onbekende droef heyd." This refers to the theological concept of the "Unknown Sufferings" of Christ—the internal, mental, and spiritual agonies that were not visible to the onlookers at the cross.</note> and the suffering that he felt, which we cannot distinctly know (as the Old Fathers of the Greek Church <note>This refers to the Eastern Orthodox tradition. The phrase mentioned is found in the "Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom" or the "Great Paraklesis," where they pray "By Thine unknown sufferings, have mercy on us."</note> said in their liturgy,) "have mercy on us, and save us." His sorrow and suffering were so great, that some have thought it is dangerous to try to depict

It specifically references the "unknown sufferings" of Christ mentioned in the Eastern Orthodox liturgical tradition.</summary>

# Heart-Theology.

...ed; but how meager and how slight is my suffering for Jesus Christ?

I have not yet resisted unto blood <note>This is a reference to Hebrews 12:4: "In your struggle against sin, you have not yet resisted to the point of shedding your blood."</note>. And even if I had, what is this in comparison to his extreme suffering? Christ died in his suffering? His patient obedience was unto the death of the Cross. He hung on the Cross until he bowed his head and gave up the spirit; He died unto Sins once.

But alas! How do I still live in the very thing for which he died? To this day, my sins have not yet given up the ghost; to this day, Christ’s death has not yet been the death of my Sins; oh! my Sins are not yet crucified; the heart-blood <note>original: "Herte-bloet." A poetic and visceral term suggesting the life-force or the very core of one's sinful nature being drained away.</note> of my sins has not yet been shed; oh! woe is me, how little am I in all these respects <term>conformed: "gelykvormig" — the spiritual process of being shaped into the likeness of Christ, particularly through sharing in his sufferings and death to sin.</term> to Christ.

->1 John 4: 9.<-

> In this the love of God toward us was revealed, that God sent his only begotten Son into the world, so that we might live through him.

<vocab>Jesus Christ, Cross, Sins, 1 John 4:9, Heart-blood, obedience, conformed</vocab>

<summary>The author laments their own spiritual inadequacy, noting that while Christ died for sin, their own sinful nature remains "uncrucified" and lively. It concludes with a scriptural reminder of God's love through the sending of His Son.</summary>
<keywords>conformity to Christ, crucifixion of sin, spiritual discipline, obedience, 1 John 4:9, suffering, atonement</keywords>

## XI. Figure.

# Here **JESUS** reigns in the Heart.

O Lord Jesus, oh how beautiful,
The Scepter and the Crown suit you!
Command, oh highest God,
We attend to your high command;
Your yoke is light, your burden is sweet <note>original: "u jok is ligt, u last is soet." This is a direct reference to Matthew 11:30: "For my yoke is easy and my burden is light."</note>,
Everything you do is Holy.
Let no difference in <term>resignation: "gelatenheyd" — a key concept in mystical devotion meaning a state of total surrender or "yielding" one's own will to God's will.</term>,
Nor own choice, nor own will,
Remain in the Heart, whether small or large;
But lead it all into death;
And cast it out as dung and mire,
Then the Heart becomes a kingdom of joy.

---

How high is such a Soul exalted,
When Jesus comes to live in the Heart;
When Jesus, God's beloved Son,
Sits there, with Scepter and Crown.
Then other lords <note>This refers to the "usurpers" of the heart: the various sins and worldly attachments that previously governed the person's life.</note> must flee;
When that King comes to command;
Yes, at his Holy Heavenly Word,
All worldly lusts must depart.
Satan, the World, the Flesh, and all,
Must fall down before his feet:
For nothing can stand before him,
When he exerts his power upon the Heart.

->*Luke 17: 21.*<-

---

> — *Neither shall they say, see here! or, see there! for the Kingdom of God is within you.*

<summary>This page presents the eleventh figure of the series, depicting Jesus as the King of the heart who displaces the "other lords" of Satan, the World, and the Flesh. It emphasizes the peace and joy found in total surrender (resignation) to Christ's gentle rule.</summary>

### XI. Figure.

## Here **Jesus** reigns in the Heart.

O Lord Jesus, oh how beautiful,
The Scepter and the Crown suit you!
Command, oh highest God,
We attend to your high command;
Your yoke is light, your burden is sweet <note>original: "u jok is ligt, u last is soet." This is a direct reference to Matthew 11:30.</note>,
Everything you do is Holy.
Let no lack of <term>resignation: "gelatenheyd" — a central term in mystical devotion meaning the total yielding of one's own will to God's will.</term>,
Nor own choice, nor own will,
Remain in the Heart, whether small or large,
But lead it all into death;
And cast it out as dung and mire <note>The author uses "drek en slyk" to emphasize that anything held onto by the "self" or "ego" is worthless compared to Christ's rule.</note>,
Then the Heart becomes a kingdom of joy.

---

How high is such a Soul exalted,
When Jesus comes to live in the Heart;
When Jesus, God's beloved Son,
Sits there, with Scepter and Crown.
Then other masters <note>original: "Heeren." Referring to the previous "lords" of the heart: sins, passions, and the self-centered ego.</note> must flee;
When that King comes to command,
Yes, at his Holy Heavenly Word,
Then all worldly desires must depart.
Satan, the World, the Flesh, and all,
Must fall down before his feet:
Nothing can withstand him,
When he exerts his power upon the Heart.

->Luke 17: 21.<-

— Neither shall they say, Look here! or, Look there! for, behold, the kingdom of God is within you.

<summary>This page illustrates the spiritual "coronation" of Jesus within the believer's heart, using the metaphor of a kingdom to describe the soul's total submission to divine will. It emphasizes that once Christ is enthroned, all other spiritual "masters"—such as sin, the devil, and the ego—are expelled.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Heart-Theology" series, moving from the visual description of the 11th emblem (Christ reigning in the heart) to a "Soul-Conversation"—a meditative dialogue where the believer reflects on the internal presence of the Divine. The previous page established Jesus as the rightful sovereign; this page explores the personal application of that kingship.</meta>

Levendige <note>The word "Levendige" likely refers to "Lively" or "Living," possibly part of a running header such as "Lively Heart-Theology."</note>

### XIX.

# Soul-Conversation.

1. What do I see here? Ah, what wonder appears here before my eyes? Do I not see the Lord Jesus sitting in the middle of the Heart, like a King on his Royal Throne, carrying Scepter and Crown?

2. Oh! how blessed and exceedingly happy is that Soul, in whose Heart Jesus has become King: for although Jesus is a King outside of your Heart, oh my soul, that cannot bring you the slightest benefit: But when Jesus has become a King within your Heart, then he is truly your King and your **Salvation** <term>salvation: "Saligheit" — in this context, it refers to both eternal life and the state of being spiritually "blessed" or "happy" in God's grace.</term>.

3. Therefore, oh faithful Savior, because you delight in ruling in the hearts of men as a reigning King, so grant me also that grace, that I present and surrender my heart to you this moment for your Throne and Palace: Ah! Occupy it, rule within it, let your dominion against your and my Enemies be made manifest there; Drive out from my heart all impure lusts and illicit desires, crush them under your feet in me: let Sin, the World, and Satan be your and my slaves and vassals.

<vocab>**Soul:** Ziele, **Heart:** Herte, **King:** Koning, **Throne:** Throon, **Salvation/Blessedness:** Saligheit, **Savior:** Heylandt, **Enemies:** Vyanden, **Satan:** Satan, **vassals:** vaffalen</vocab>

<meta>catchword: 4. Wild</meta>

<summary>A meditative reflection on the 11th emblem of the Heart-Theology series, emphasizing that Christ's kingship is only beneficial if it is internal and personal. The text concludes with a prayer for Christ to occupy the heart and defeat spiritual enemies like sin and the devil.</summary>
<keywords>Soul-Conversation, Jesus as King, internal devotion, spiritual warfare, salvation, heart-theology, Pietism</keywords>

Heart-Theology.

4. Do not then cease, oh my Soul, until you obtain this King in your Heart as a Ruler: oh! how blessed you shall be, when this King shall reign in you? How shall you then be able to be a Lord and Master over Sin, Satan, and the World? But unblessed and exceedingly wretched shall you be, both in this and in the coming Age <note>original: "Eeuwe"—referring to the present life and the eternal world to come.</note>; when Jesus does not become King in your Heart, but instead Lucifer <note>A name for the Devil, representing the prideful opposition to Christ's rule.</note> with the wicked, evil World remain reigning therein: ah! what a wretched Slave <note>The original Dutch uses the feminine form "Slavinne" because the Soul (Ziele) is grammatically feminine.</note> they shall make of you, both here and hereafter.

## Sighing of the Soul.

> O King Jesus, God’s Son,
> Truly make my heart your Throne,
> So that you may reign therein,
> Then I have rest, then I have joy,
> Then I am fit once again,
> Always to praise your name.

### XIX.

## *Soul-Conversation.*

1. What do I see here? Ah, what wonder appears here before my eyes? Do I not see the Lord Jesus <note>original: "Jefum," the Latin accusative form of Jesus often used in Dutch devotional writing.</note> sitting in the center of the Heart, like a King on his Royal Throne, bearing Scepter and Crown.

2. Oh! how blessed and exceedingly happy is that Soul, in whose Heart Jesus has become King: for although Jesus is a King outside of your Heart, O my Soul, that cannot bring you the least bit of benefit: But when Jesus has become a King inside your Heart, then he is truly your King and your Salvation.

3. Therefore, O faithful Savior, since you delight to reign in the hearts of men as a ruling King, grant me also the grace that I present and surrender my heart to you this moment as your Throne and Palace: Ah! take possession of it, reign within it, let your dominion be made manifest therein against your and my Enemies; Drive out from my heart all impure lusts and unlawful desires, crush them under your feet within me: let Sin, the World, and Satan be your and my slaves and vassals.

<meta>catchword: 4. Wilt (translated as "4. Do not" or "4. Will" on the following page)</meta>

But unhappy and exceedingly miserable you shall be, both in this and in the coming Age <note>original: "Eeuwe," meaning age or eternity.</note>; when Jesus does not become King in your heart, but instead Lucifer with the wicked, evil world continues to reign therein: ah! what a wretched slave <note>original: "Slavinne," the feminine form of slave, as the soul (Ziele) is grammatically feminine in Dutch.</note> they shall make of you, here and hereafter.

## XI. Figure.

Rejoice, you people, all together,
For Jesus has His Kingdom;
The glory of His Holy light,
Has been established in the human heart.
Command now, Lord, we remain still,
To do everything according to Your will;
Your Word is so powerfully sweet to us,
That whoever hears it, must praise You.
Praise be to God who, in Your hand,
Has planted the Scepter of David; <note>The "Scepter of David" refers to the biblical promise of an eternal messianic kingdom (see 2 Samuel 7).</note>
And who has promised the Crown of Glory,
To You for all eternity.

---

->When the Lord God establishes the Holy Kingdom<-
->of Christ His Son in the hearts of men,<-
->Then it makes the person desire all that is in their Lord’s heart,<-
->And at last, they receive the joyful Crown of Heaven.<-

---

No joy can represent this joy,
When Jesus is King in the Heart;
Then the Soul swims in full abundance, <note>original: "weelden," meaning luxury, spiritual riches, or abundance.</note>
And is freed from pressure and pain:
Then the Soul sits in quietness,
And allows her King, Crown, and Staff,
To trample upon all sins,
And cast them into the dreariest grave.

# Further Observation
## From J. TAULER, <note>Johannes Tauler (c. 1300–1361) was a famous German Dominican mystic and preacher whose writings deeply influenced later Pietist and contemplative traditions.</note>
### *On the XI. Figure.*

Those people who have entered into this internal Kingdom through the true door, Jesus Christ, find pure truth, which is unknown to all people who do not dwell in this ground <note>Tauler uses the term "ground" (German: *Grund*) to refer to the deepest, most inward part of the soul where man meets God.</note>. These people find what Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist whose mystical writings emphasized that God is found beyond all human intellect and "divine darkness."</note> writes: that which is above all ingenuity, above all thoughts, and above all understanding; that is, they find that light within the light.

The Doctors and the learned read books with diligence and turn the pages, but these people read that true living Book, in which everything lives. For they turn over the Heavens and the Earth and read therein the excellent great wonders of God, and proceed further to the distinction of the dear Angels of God, and come further still to the supreme letter of the Holy worthy Trinity: namely, how God the Father has eternally begotten His Son Jesus Christ, and eternally bears Him, and how that eternal Word has played eternally in His Fatherly Heart <note>original: "geſpeeld," referring to the *Ludus Amoris* or "Divine Play," a mystical concept describing the joyful, spontaneous interaction within the Trinity.</note>, and how God the Holy Spirit always flows from them both, and how the Holy, High Trinity always pours itself out into all Blessed Spirits, and how they pour themselves back in actual, eternal Bliss. This is the Bliss of which the Son of God, Jesus Christ, spoke: *Father, this is eternal life, that they might know You, the only true God, and Jesus Christ, whom You have sent.* <note>original: "Vader dat is het eeuwige leven..." A translation of John 17:3.</note> Children, this is the true life in the internal Temple of God; here sits the supreme King in His Palace. Whoever has experienced <note>original: "verſogt," meaning to have tasted, tested, or experienced through trial.</note> this knows it, and no one else.

<summary>This page features a commentary by the mystic Johannes Tauler on the "inward kingdom," contrasting the intellectual study of scholars with the direct, experiential "reading" of God's wonders and the Trinity within the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Tauler, Dionysius the Areopagite, Inward Kingdom, Trinity, Divine Play, Ground of the Soul, Mysticism, John 17:3</keywords>

You would joyfully leave your earthly palace,
For to serve Him is to reign, <note>This is a classic Christian paradox: *cui servire regnare est* (to serve whom is to reign), suggesting that spiritual submission to God grants the believer true sovereignty over themselves and the world.</note>

Which becomes a fountain within him,

### XII. Figure.

## Here **JESUS** rests in the Heart.

What can indeed such a Heart harm, <note>original: "deere." An archaic Dutch verb meaning to harm, injure, or cause grief.</note>
When Jesus the most beautiful Lord,
Sits down in the Heart and rests,
And gives the Soul its joy's delight.
Even if Satan with his companions, <note>original: "boelen." Literally "paramours" or "lovers," used here pejoratively to describe the demonic spirits or temptations that accompany Satan.</note>
Swerve and swarm all around;
It may do no harm, the great God
Rests in the Heart, the Soul’s stronghold. <note>original: "slot." This term can mean a "lock," a "conclusion," or a "castle/stronghold." In this context, it suggests that God is both the inhabitant of the heart's fortress and the lock that keeps it secure.</note>
Even though the fierce lightning bolts,
Descend into the deepest valley of the Heart;
What harm can that do, when beautiful Jesus!
Sits there and rests, and has his Throne.
What can the wind of misfortune harm,
Upon the Heart, where the Lord rests within:
What can the Cross <note>original: "het Kruys." In 17th-century devotional language, "the Cross" refers to personal suffering, trials, or burdens borne by a believer.</note> do, or the smarting pain,
So long as the King sits in the Heart.
No unrest can such a Soul disturb,
Whatever it may be, that comes to hear,
The voice of Jesus in his Heart,
No matter how he is tossed about.
His eyes are fixed upon him,
In every Cross, and if you should ask;
Where he gets that peace and rest from?
It is because Jesus lives in him.

->Jᴏʜɴ 14: 20.<-

->*and you in me, and I in you.*<-

# XII.

### *Soul-Dialogue.*

1. **A**lthough all the storm-winds and raging waves of the restless sea of this wild world move against me, they shall not prevail against me, when you, O good Jesus, will be pleased to possess my heart through your Spirit.

2. Oh! That I might so encounter, serve, and thank you, that I might keep you in my heart, so that with you resting there, I should need to fear no misfortune, because you—my Savior <term>Heyland: Literally 'one who heals' or 'makes whole,' a common Dutch title for Christ as the Great Physician of the soul.</term>, Redeemer, and Bringer of Salvation <note>original: "Saligmaker." From the Dutch 'zalig,' meaning blessed or blissful; specifically one who brings the soul to a state of eternal bliss.</note>—are with me, in me, yes, in the very midst of my heart.

3. For you, my Preserver <note>original: "Behouder." A term for a protector or one who keeps something safe from harm.</note>, are my Light in my darknesses and in the wandering paths of this evil world; who then will or can mislead me, so long as you are with me and are my Guardian? You are my strength in my weakness—who then can overcome me? You are my joy in my sadness—who then can grieve me? You are my eternal Life—who then can separate me from you?

4. Well then, my soul, apply yourself at all <note>The text cuts off here. The catchword "len" suggests the next page begins with "t’allen tijden," meaning "at all times."</note>

<summary>A devotional dialogue where the soul addresses Jesus as its internal protector, declaring that His presence in the heart provides immunity against the "storms" and "waves" of a chaotic world. The passage emphasizes Christ's roles as Light, Strength, and Joy that dwell within the believer.</summary>
<keywords>soul-dialogue, indwelling of Christ, spiritual peace, Savior, internal sanctuary, divine protection</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Soul-Dialogue" from page 106, completing the sentence regarding the soul's commitment to Christ at all times.</meta>

<header>Heart Theology.</header>

times, so that you may have and keep this Bridegroom <term>Bruydegom: A mystical reference to Christ as the spouse of the soul, a common theme in contemplative and pietist literature based on the Song of Solomon.</term> of yours with you, so that He might be your only salvation against all your adversities and temptations <note>original: "aanvechtingen." This refers to spiritual assaults, inner turmoil, or trials of faith.</note>; He shall be all things to you, against all things, for He is indeed all in all.

5. It is true that it sometimes seems as if the Lord were sleeping within you, when He does not always work something in you as quickly as you might wish; but that is His testing, necessary and useful for your trial and spiritual exercise <note>original: "oeffeninge." This refers to the disciplined practice of the inner life and the refining of the soul's character.</note>, so that you should call to Him all the more fervently, inwardly, and earnestly.

6. When, therefore, evil influences and impulses begin to rise within you, and Jesus seems to be sleeping, then it is high time that you seek to awaken Him through earnest sighs <note>original: "zuchtingen." In 17th-century devotion, a 'sigh' or 'groan' was seen as a profound, wordless prayer that bypasses the intellect to reach God directly.</note> and move Him to help you.

<header>Living</header>

### XII. Figure. <note>In 17th-century devotional books, a "Figure" (Figuur) was an emblem—a symbolic illustration—accompanied by a poem that explained its spiritual meaning. This poem likely accompanied an image of a heart with Jesus resting peacefully inside it.</note>

O sweet rest, who can disturb you,
Though heaven strikes blow upon blow,
Though the abyss <note>original: "afgrond." This refers to hell or the depths of spiritual chaos.</note> roars as loud as it can,
Though the winds let themselves be heard;
It is all in vain, however things go,
When it stands thus within the Heart.
No creature can disturb the place,
On which its Creator takes delight,
Resting lovely in deep silence,
Even if all creatures conspired against it:
Though there rains down an abundance
Of strife, and Cross <note>original: "Kruys." In this context, the 'Cross' represents the personal burdens, sufferings, and trials a Christian must bear.</note>, and adversity;
Inwardly the rest remains in essence,
And knows of neither fright nor fear.

What use to a soul is much money and goods,
What can all his wealth give him,
What nobility of the highest blood;
What status in this mortal life?
If he has no rest in Jesus,
And if his Soul can but experience,
That all that floats before the eyes;
Fleet time always comes to devour.
It is vanity <note>original: "ydelheid." A reference to the Book of Ecclesiastes, meaning something fleeting, empty, or without lasting substance.</note>; earthly goods
Can never give peace to the soul,
But Jesus resting in the mind <note>original: "gemoet." A term for the inner spirit, disposition, or the seat of emotions and thought.</note>;
Gives to the soul a better life.

->EPHESIANS 2, VERSE 14.<-

<header>Living</header>

## Sighs of the Soul.

Oh! if only we had reached that state, where we had become our Lord's property; we would be released from all care, we would enjoy everything a Christian may wish for, yes, all the treasures and riches of God's mercy would be open to us; in Hell and in death we would find the Highest Good, <note>original: "Hoogste Goed." This is the Dutch translation of the Latin *Summum Bonum*, a philosophical and theological term for the ultimate importance or God as the source of all happiness.</note> that is God.

> Even if Heaven storms so fiercely,
> Even if the lightning shoots so fast,
> There is no squall, nor thunderclap,
> That may disturb me in my heart;
> All that rages outside is nothing,
> As long as one feels peace within:
> When the Lord of Grace upon His bed
> Has settled Himself to His peaceful rest;
> For where He sits, there the Spirit is
> Unafraid within the human heart.

> When Jesus has lain down to rest in your Soul,
> Everything that is outside does not harm the person,
> Though adversity storms, though the rain of temptation falls,
> That Soul where Jesus rests, nothing harms her at all.

->PSALM 27: 1.<-

*The Lord is my light and my salvation; whom shall I fear? the Lord is the strength of my life; of whom shall I be afraid?*

<summary>This page presents "Sighs" (devotional yearnings) and poetry focused on the total security found when the soul becomes God's property. It emphasizes that inner peace, granted by Jesus's presence, renders external storms and temptations harmless.</summary>
<keywords>inner peace, divine providence, Psalm 27:1, Highest Good, spiritual rest, security in God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual reflections on inner peace from page 110. While the previous page focused on the "Sighs of the Soul" regarding the Lord's presence within, this page provides a "Further Reflection" on a specific illustration (the 12th Figure), contrasting the fleeting nature of the material world with the eternal rest found in God.</meta>

<header>Heart-Theology.</header>

## Further Reflection
# On the 12th Figure. <note>The "Figure" refers to a specific illustration in this emblem book—a popular 17th-century genre where a symbolic image was paired with a moral or religious lesson.</note>

Those people who have their portion in the world, and who seek their rest in things which perish with time and vanish through use, are the poorest and most wretched creatures in the world; every creature <note>original: "schepsel." In this context, it refers to any created thing—whether animal, plant, or object—rather than just a living being.</note> in the world calls out to us, as it were, that no true rest is to be found within them, and therefore we may say: this is not the place of our rest.

Our souls are created by God for a higher contemplation of things which can satisfy our immortal souls, and because this satisfaction is not to be found in any created thing, we must therefore seek it in Him who is the creator and former of all that is created.

Our rest must then be in God, and He in us, then our souls shall find joy and gladness in Him; for when God fills the soul, then all other joy and satisfaction must give way.

Oh my soul, seek then your rest in Jesus alone, seek your Highest Good <note>original: "Hoogste goet," referring to the *Summum Bonum*, the ultimate importance or God as the source of all happiness.</note> in Him; for His loving-kindness is better than life itself: do not let your soul be so burdened with these vanities of the world that you would leave no place for Jesus in your heart; but seek to keep Him above all else in your heart.

->PSALM 132: 8.<-

> Arise, O Lord, into Thy rest; Thou, and the ark of Thy strength.

## XIII. Figure.

### Here Jesus sings in the Heart.

It must compel the Soul to joy,
When Jesus comes to sing in the Heart,
When Jesus, God's beloved Son,
Raises up a Heavenly tone therein;
Who can proclaim <note>original: "uytgalmen," meaning to echo forth or announce loudly.</note> that great joy,
When Jesus sings Psalms in the Heart
Of all his great love,
And so compels us to his love.
O Savior! King! and Ruler!
Raise your Heavenly tone all the more,
In the Heart of a dead soul, <note>In 17th-century theology, a "dead soul" refers to a person before spiritual awakening or one feeling spiritually dry.</note>
From whom you never withheld your favor;
Truly there can never be joy,
If you do not come to heal the Soul,
And pour into her your joy and light,
And show your friendly countenance;
O Human! if you wish to taste joys,
That touch you even to the Heart,
It is Jesus's Divine Spirit,
Which makes a Heavenly feast in the Heart;
O, could you obtain this joy;
You would hear the songs of Jesus,
Which he continually, through his Spirit and Word,
Brings forth sweetly in the Holy Heart;
This joy can be measured,
When the sanctified conscience <note>original: "geweten." The conscience was viewed as the internal seat of God's judgment and peace.</note>
Rests most sweetly, and tastes that treasure
Which the world does not grasp.

### Here Jesus sings in the Heart.

The Soul must be compelled to joy,
When Jesus comes to sing in the Heart,
When Jesus, God’s beloved Son,
Raises therein a Heavenly tone.

When Jesus sings Psalms in the Heart
Of all His great and wondrous love,

Raise Your Heavenly tone even more,
In the Heart of a dead soul, <note>In this context, "dead soul" refers to a person who is spiritually dormant or unawakened before divine grace touches them.</note>

Unless You come to heal the Soul,

And show Your kind countenance.
O Mankind! If you wish to taste joys
That touch you even to the Heart,
It is Jesus’s Divine Spirit
Which creates a Heavenly feast in the Heart.
O, if you could attain this joy;
You would hear Jesus’s Songs,
Which He, through His Spirit and Word,
Always brings forth sweetly in the Holy Heart.
This joy can be measured
When the sanctified conscience
Rests very sweetly, and tastes that treasure
Which the world does not comprehend.

### XIII. Figure.

When all creatures <note>In 17th-century spiritual writing, "creatures" refers to all created things—people, animals, and worldly distractions—rather than just animals.</note> were silent,
And the heart had found a space,
So that solitude occupied the place,
And drove all the turmoil out the door,
Then, when all comforts failed him,
He heard Jesus singing within him:
  O Sweet song of God’s Son,
  You are far too beautiful for man.
Then the heart could forget all joy,
That was not granted by God.

---

Who can understand these high things?
When Jesus comes to compel the heart,
  And gives therein a heavenly word,
  So that joy is heard:
The soul itself, in much oppression,
Is cheerful in her misfortunes,
  For Jesus’ joy rests in the heart;
  Which makes all her pain forgotten.
O Son of God! let me always hear,
Your voice which can charm the angels,
  Your voice, and language so exceedingly sweet,
  So comforting for a weak spirit. <note>original: "gemmoet" – refers to the inner mind, disposition, or soul.</note>

*they shall rejoice in Your name the whole day;*
*and be exalted through Your righteousness.*

### XIII

Play upon the strings, sound out joyfully,
For Jesus sings the music;
Let everyone’s instrument from now on,
Go forth in praise to Jesus’s tones.
Let now the great host of Angels,
And also all the Saints;
Whether on Cymbal, <note>original: "Cimbaal" – in the 17th century, this could refer to a variety of instruments including a dulcimer or a psaltery, not just modern cymbals.</note> on Harp, or Flute,
Send their song of thanks to this sound:
Thus shall the joy from now on,
Exist into eternity.

When Jesus awakens joy in the Heart of man,
Then man may rightly leap up for joy,
When Jesus sets the tone, of love undefiled,
Man may joyfully sing the praise of the Lord.

No joy is to be compared to this joy,
When Jesus is highly praised,
The Soul is constantly strengthened by his powers;
This outshines <note>original: "verdooft" – literally "extinguishes" or "deadens," used here to mean that the divine joy makes all earthly pleasures fade into insignificance.</note> all other joy.
When that beautiful Sun comes to shine
In the dark mind, <note>original: "gemoet" – a central term in Dutch spirituality referring to the inner soul, disposition, or seat of the emotions.</note>
Which makes the darkness disappear,
And gives his Heavenly sweetness:
Oh Joy of God’s Son!
Oh! may you always dwell in me.

# XIV. Figure.

## Here Jesus plays in the Heart.

A joy that never reaches the ears,
Which man neither tastes nor savors,
With all that touches the body,
Is born by God within the Soul.
Where does the world remain with its joy,
Which gladdens nothing but the flesh?
The sweet voice of Jesus’ strings, <note>The "strings" refer to the internal faculties of the heart, which Jesus tunes and plays like a musical instrument to produce spiritual harmony.</note>
Which make the Heart so that it sings,
And as if drowns in His love;
O Son of God, please reveal,
More and more within my spirit, <note>original: "gemoet" — the seat of one's inner thoughts, feelings, and spiritual disposition.</note>
The joy of your Heavenly glow.
Who, Lord, will be able to keep silent,
When You give the Heart through Yourself,
An invitation to that Divine Heavenly feast,
Which few here on earth obtain:
O Fairest Lord! O dearest friend!
How blessed is he who finds You.
Let others rejoice in play,
In vanity, and delicacies, <note>original: "lekkerny" — refers here to worldly indulgences and physical pleasures that distract from spiritual growth.</note>
I set all this aside,
To rejoice with Jesus.
This Divine delight of the Soul,
Is the only thing for which I long:
O dear Jesus, beloved of my Soul,
Grant that I may find it with You.

# XVI. Figure.

## Here Jesus plays in the Heart.

<image-desc>An emblem engraving featuring a large central heart. Inside the heart, a youthful Jesus is standing on a cloud, playing a harp. Above the heart, the Holy Spirit appears as a dove descending towards a funnel-like opening at the top. Four angels are positioned on clouds in the corners: the two at the top are reading books, while the two at the bottom look toward the central figure. A rectangular text box at the bottom of the image contains a Dutch biblical reference.</image-desc>

> David took the harp and he played
> with his hand and that was a change
> for Saul and he was bettered
> and the evil spirit departed from him. <note>A vernacular translation of 1 Samuel 16:23. In this mystical context, King Saul represents the restless, troubled soul, while David represents the Christ-figure who brings divine order through music.</note>

It is the true Joy of Heaven,
When Jesus gladdens the human heart,
And plays upon its deified strings; <note>original: "vergoode ſnaaren" — This suggests the heart's internal faculties have been transformed or "made divine" by grace, allowing them to produce a harmony pleasing to God.</note>
He can somewhat explain it—
That friend, who experiences
The love of Jesus.

# XIV. Figure.

## ->Here JESUS plays in the Heart.<-

A joy that never reaches the ears,
  Which man neither tastes nor savors,
  With all that concerns the body,
Is born by God within the Soul.
  Where does the world remain with its joy,
  Which does nothing but delight the flesh?
The sweet voice of Jesus’ strings, <note>A common mystical metaphor where the heart's virtues or "faculties" are compared to the strings of a harp or lute, tuned and played upon by Christ.</note>
  That cause the Heart to sing,
  And almost drown in His love;
O Son of God, may You reveal,
  More and more within my spirit,
  The joy of Your heavenly glow.
Who shall, O Lord, be able to keep silent,
  When You give Yourself through the Heart,
  Inviting to that Divine Heaven-feast,
That few here on earth obtain:
  O fairest Lord! O dearest friend!
  How blessed is he who finds You.
Let others in their games rejoice,
  In vanity and delicacies,
  I set all this aside,
To rejoice myself with Jesus.
  This Divine delight of the Soul,
  Is the only thing for which I long:
  O dear Jesus, my Soul’s beloved,
  Grant that I may find it with You.

->SONG OF SOLOMON 7:6.<- <note>original: "HOOGL. 7: 6." — *Hooglied* (High Song) is the Dutch name for the Song of Solomon or Canticles.</note>

*How beautiful you are, and how pleasant you are, O love! in delights.*

### XIV.

## Conversation of the Soul.

1. O Lord, most adorable goodness; who has the capacity to express, with his human understanding, this great mystery, which is not heard with ears, nor seen with eyes, nor touched with hands, nor can be tasted with the tongue; but that is only tasted and felt in the innermost ground <note>original: "binnensten grond." A key term in mystical theology referring to the deepest, most essential part of the human spirit where man meets God.</note> of the souls of your lovers.

2. O Jesus! you fountain of joy and gladness, please gladden my soul continually through your pleasant sound of the strings of your Spirit; make me hear that voice of joys and gladness which you wish to give and bestow upon those who seek you with their whole heart.

3. O take away from me all that can hinder, let your Divine work be in my heart, so that it may be entirely and completely for you; and that you might let your Heavenly voice be heard continually in my soul; so that the heart may be all the more turned away from the trivial and vain things of this World; O come, you blessed Son of God, and live eternally in my heart.

### Sighing of the Soul.
<note>original: "Suchtinge der Ziele." In 17th-century devotional practice, a "sigh" was a short, poetic aspiration or prayer intended to lift the heart toward God.</note>

## XIV. Figure.
<note>In 17th-century devotional literature, a "Figure" or "Emblem" was an illustration accompanied by a poem and scripture, intended to guide the reader in meditation.</note>

There is a ground in man’s Spirit,
That never yet has been imagined;
No <term>Creature</term> <note>Original: "Creatuur." In this context, it refers to any created being—human, angelic, or animal—as opposed to the Creator.</note> can approach there,
But God alone can strike that chord;
And when God touches man there,
Then a great joy must arise;
No joy through images nor through senses
From without, and carried within;
But joy through Divine power,
Produced in the depths of the Soul.
In the Divine image, in the Soul’s essence,
Risen up like a flowing spring,
That is the Harp, which Jesus plays,
That surpasses all joy;
Then the heart laughs at all the riches,
Which the World can imagine for itself.

->ROMANS 14: 17.<-

*For the Kingdom of God is not food and drink, but righteousness and peace, and joy through the Holy Spirit.*

->HABAKKUK 3: 18.<-

*Yet I will nonetheless leap for joy in the Lord: I will rejoice in the God of my salvation.*

# S O N G :

## Psalm 78.
<note>While titled "Psalm 78," this text is a lyrical paraphrase or a devotional poem inspired by themes of God's guidance and the soul's history, rather than a literal translation of the biblical Psalm.</note>

->*On the* XIV. *Figure*.<-

O Great Lord! and God of all ages,
To whom my Soul in Cross, in pain and suffering
Sends its sighs, so that you might hear me
From your Throne; oh, lend me your ears,
And let your Spirit, the <term>medicine of souls</term> <note>Original: "Zielen medicyn." A common metaphor in 17th-century Pietism, viewing the Holy Spirit as a healer for the "sickness" of sin and despair.</note>,
For my comfort, always be active within me.

Let, Lord! your Spirit sent from above,
Proclaim your love and pure favor to me,
So that the soul through your love may live,
When you have given yourself to the heart,
You strike the **Harp of your joy** in the heart,
So that the soul may be united with you.

So that she hears your voice, who can describe it?
O beautiful Jesus! as those who placed themselves before you,
Stripped of that which can harm such love,
How do I obtain God, that most beautiful Lord?
How do I have Him again with His lovely playing,
In my mind? Who [longs] neither for earth nor Hell. <note>This expresses the mystical state of "disinterest" or "detachment," where the soul is so focused on God that it is indifferent to both worldly rewards and the fear of punishment.</note>

Oh, do come back! O most beautiful one, let yourself be found;
And be not like a <term>fawn of the deer</term> <note>Original: "welp der Hinden." This is a poetic reference to the Song of Solomon (2:9, 2:17, 8:14), where the Divine Beloved is compared to a swift gazelle or young stag that leaps over mountains and is often elusive.</note>,
That swiftly flees, and do not leave me forsaken,
While my soul lies cast down by anguish,
O dear Jesus! come back, and do not tarry,
And let my soul no longer [remain] in sorrow.

<gloss>Heart-Theology</gloss>, <gloss>Psalm 78</gloss>, <gloss>Song</gloss>, <gloss>Soul's medicine</gloss>, <gloss>Jesus</gloss>, <gloss>Harp of your joy</gloss>

<summary>This page contains a devotional poem (Song) intended for meditation on the "14th Figure." It depicts a soul pleading for the Holy Spirit's comfort and for Jesus to return and play the "Harp of joy" within the heart, using imagery from the Song of Solomon.</summary>
<keywords>devotional poetry, Holy Spirit, mystical union, Song of Solomon imagery, Jesus as musician, spiritual healing</keywords>

When you, Lord, strike the Harp,
Which stands strung within the heart,
Then everything sings with pure joy,
Whatever rejoices in **God’s Praise**,
Strike, Jesus, let your sweet sound ring out,
So that even the angels break forth <note>Meaning the angels join in the song of the soul.</note>,
And give more joy in a single day,
Than man may ever imagine.

The joy and gladness that God gives,
While one still lives upon the Earth,
Is for a soul impossible to express;
They cause the heart to be pulled upward,
So that a person <term>sinks, as it were, into God</term> <note>Original: "in God als ſinkt." This describes "unio mystica" or mystical union, where the individual self is perceived to dissolve into the divine presence.</note>,
And drinks from that blessed wellspring.
O joy! and gladness, what are you?
How you do make the soul so happy;
It is like a pang to the soul, when the rays
Of the **Divine light** withdraw their brilliance;
There the soul suffers, parched and dead,
Alas! deprived of the blessed light,
And knows not where to turn,
It must go without the greatest joy;
That creates in its dejected heart
A woe! and the very greatest pain:
It cannot rightly be proclaimed,
Except by those who have experienced it themselves.

# XIV.

He who wishes to hear the blessed voice of Jesus
Within his spirit, let him stop his ears
To all that the World says,
No matter how it wheedles and flatters:
When that Sun <note>A common metaphor for Christ as the "Sun of Righteousness."</note> wishes to let His rays
Descend into the heart of man,
Then everything must melt away to nothing
Whereon the heart still gazes <note>Meaning the heart must turn away from all worldly attachments to focus solely on the divine.</note>,
All **selfhood** <term>selfhood</term> <note>Original: "eygenheyd." In Pietist theology, this refers to the selfish will or ego that must be destroyed (mortified) to make room for God.</note> must be brought to shame,
When Jesus comes to set the heart ablaze,
When Jesus bores through the soul,
And raises in the heart a beautiful harmony <note>This "harmony" or "accord" refers back to the "Harp of Jesus" imagery from the previous page.</note>,
Who shall stand before such a Lord?
Who shall turn his heart away from Him?
Who shall not love such a friend,
In whom the soul finds its delight?
The World may boast of its joys,
Of all its pleasure and its **vain flowers**,
Its joy and desires which perish,
While the others <note>The spiritual joys.</note> shall stand eternally;
Let all [earthly] joy freely depart from me,
So long as God’s joy does not falter,
And God, who grants all joy,
Makes me gladder than anyone can imagine.

->ISAIAH 61:10.<-

> I am greatly joyful in the Lord, my soul rejoices
> in the God of my salvation.

No worlds full of abundance,

No silver, gold, nor anything in life,

Who has His dwelling in the heart,

# XIV. Figure.

Lift your voice, O holy hosts! <note>Original: "Heyl’ge scharen." Likely referring to the company of angels or the communion of saints.</note>
The Son of God plays on His strings,
In the Heart that now lives for Him,
And gives itself wholly and entirely to Him.
In the Heart that is found pure,
Cleansed through His blood and wounds;
In the Heart, where He dwells and lives,
And that surrenders itself to Him.

To Heaven, before the blessed eyes,
And enters into Heaven in such a way,
Where she tastes her Jesus’ love. <note>Original: "min." An archaic word for love, often used in mystical poetry to describe the intimate bond between the soul and God.</note>
Away with you, O world with your lusts!
Could you indeed rest in that greatest beauty;

With which Jesus gladdens her?
O No! your eyes are too dark,

With all your treasures, and your honor,
Obtain never, nor forevermore.

## XV. Figure.

### Here JESUS strews the Heart with Roses.

Hold still, O soul, hold still: and see
Is this not the true Heaven?
When Jesus, God most blessed,
The Sun of righteousness; <note>A reference to Malachi 4:2, often used in mystical literature to describe Christ's illumination of the soul.</note>
Rose in the Heart: then has His power,
Brought forth many beautiful little flowers:
He plucks, and strews on every side,
Prepares His bed with His own hand,
O sweet Jesus! most beautiful Lord,
Now lay Yourself down upon those little flowers;
Now lay down for sweet rest,
In the Heart You have Your delight;
You adorn it with a crown of roses, <note>Original: "roofen hoed," literally a "rose hat," but in this period it refers to a garland or wreath worn on the head, symbolizing victory or bridal joy.</note>
Dipped in Your wounded blood;
With what should we, the unlovely,
Reward Your love?

# XV. Figure.

## Soul-Dialogue.

1. This Figure, O most blessed and most holy Savior <note>Dutch: *Saligmaker*, literally "One who makes blessed," a traditional title for Christ</note>, shows, oh, how You wish to fill the heart of Your believers with all joys and gladness—yes, with such joy that surpasses the understanding of the people of the world, however deep and high they may be instructed in the outward letter of the Scripture. <note>The author distinguishes between "the outward letter" (academic or literal knowledge) and the internal spiritual experience of faith.</note>

2. Who can, O Lord, express Your gladness, which You give to a soul that lies down surrendered and naked <note>The terms "surrendered" (*gelaaten*) and "naked" (*bloot*) are key mystical concepts referring to a state of total detachment from worldly desires and self-will</note> before You, and who is as if surrounded by You, and led into such things that no eye has seen, nor any ear has heard. <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 2:9 regarding the hidden things of God.</note>

3. O my Soul! How you ought to seek to nurture and maintain Jesus’s joys; you must be able to despise all worldly joys and vanities for that reason, and esteem God’s gladness and joy more than all the lusts, all the joys and the delight of this world.

4. O my Soul! Do, like Moses, regard the treasures of Egypt as nothing <note>A reference to Hebrews 11:24–26, where Moses chooses spiritual suffering over the "treasures in Egypt"</note>, but the same—

# *Further Observation*
## Upon the 15th Figure,
### From AMBROSIUS. <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 339–397), a Bishop of Milan and one of the most influential ecclesiastical figures of the 4th century, known for his writings on ethics and mysticism.</note>

Consider the might, power, and influence of the most perfect object, Jesus, seated in Heaven at the right hand of His Father, sending His joy of the Holy Spirit into the Hearts of His Children: Oh! What a noble tower have we erected here to look toward Heaven? Faith can, as it were, stand upon this mountain and see itself in Glory.

O the floods! The rich streams of grace and Glory that flow forth from here! Come, let us draw water, for the well is deep; all those drops and the dew that fall upon men or Angels are but as splinters <note>original: *spaanders*; used here metaphorically to mean tiny, insignificant fragments</note> in comparison to that great and infinite body of grace that is in Christ. A lily is nothing at all compared to a broad and boundless field of lilies; Christ is, in these respects, the mountain of roses; oh, how high, how broad, how full, how glorious, how green! If only you could smell Him who grazes among the lilies, until the day breaks and the shadows flee. <note>A reference to the Song of Solomon 2:16-17, a common source for mystical imagery regarding the union of the soul and Christ.</note>

Could we but dig into those golden veins of these unsearchable riches of Jesus Christ, we would say: it is good that we [are] here <note>This sentence ends abruptly as it leads into the next page; it alludes to the words of Peter at the Transfiguration (Matthew 17:4).</note>

It invites the reader to contemplate Christ’s heavenly position and the spiritual riches flowing from Him to the believer's heart.</summary>

## XV. Figure.

O Dear child! how is your bed
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With briar-roses <note>original: *dooren-roos*; likely referring to the sweetbrier or wild rose, combining the beauty of the flower with the sharp thorns of the Passion.</note> so beset?
What loveliness have you
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the heart of man prepared?
Scatter all your blossoms here and there,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And lay yourself down in my Heart;
Rejoice in that sweet power,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which you yourself have brought about.

When Jesus gladdens the Heart,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then the Soul is entirely full of joy,
And knows not how to speak of love,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Until her Jesus comes to break the heart;
Then from the mouth [comes] that which in the heart
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Was hidden: oh sweet pain! <note>original: *foete fmert*; a classic oxymoron in mystical literature describing the "wound of love" where the soul finds pleasure even in the painful longing for God.</note>
O Love of God’s sweet Son!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How you thus show your love:
O Sun of righteousness! <note>original: *Sonne der geregtigheyd*; a traditional title for Christ derived from Malachi 4:2.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Illumine my Soul, from time to time;
Give me your heaven-joy in the Heart,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which shall never cause me pain,
For your love, exceedingly great,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Overcomes Hell, yes even death;
Oh joy and love never praised enough!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How sweet it is to be in you.

## XV. Figure.

O Dear child! how is your bed
With briar-roses <note>original: *dooren-roos*; likely referring to the wild rose, symbolizing both beauty and the "thorns" of Christ's future passion.</note> so surrounded?
What loveliness have you,
Prepared within the human heart?
Scatter all your little flowers here and there,
And lay yourself down in my Heart;
Rejoice in that sweet power,
Which you yourself have brought about.

Whenever Jesus gladdens the Heart,
Then the Soul is entirely full of joy,
And knows not how to speak of love,
Until her Jesus comes to break the heart;
Then from the mouth [comes] that which in the heart
Was hidden: oh sweet pain! <note>original: *foete fmert*; a classic mystical oxymoron describing the "sweet" suffering of divine longing.</note>
O Love of God’s sweet Son!
How you come thus to show your love:
O Sun of righteousness! <note>A common title for Christ derived from Malachi 4:2.</note>
Shine upon my Soul, from time to time;
Give me your Heaven-joy in the Heart,
That shall never cause me pain,
For your love, so exceedingly great,
Overcomes Hell, yes even death;
Ah, joy and love never praised enough!
How sweet it is to be in you.

## XVI.

### Here JESUS wounds the Heart.

After you, great God, have set my Soul free
From smart, from anxiety, and pain, and Satan’s evil power,
So you desire to wound my sighing Heart as if with arrows,
Which you, O dear Jesus! have placed upon your bow.
Wound my Heart freely, O dear Son of God!
Shoot through my Heart freely, with arrows heavy or light,
My soul shall show gratitude to you for that, Lord;
When you help the Soul, then all things are light <note>The author uses a play on words: "ligt" means both "light" (not heavy) and "easy."</note> for her.
Wound my Heart freely, if it be your pleasure,
After you give strength, O Lord, within my mind! <note>original: *gemoet*; refers to one's inner disposition, spirit, or emotional state.</note>
So that I may be more released from sin,
And love you, O most beautiful Lord! as the highest good. <note>original: *hoogste goet*; a reference to the theological concept of the *Summum Bonum*, the ultimate importance of God as the source of all goodness.</note>
Your Arrows, descended deep into the Heart,
Do indeed cause the Soul woe for a time;
But if you only show your countenance, then the pain is
As if it had never been, entirely vanished.
Oh, do not fear then, O Soul! who in the midst of your suffering,
Sometimes sees God’s Hand, armed with his bow,
It is indeed the way Home, through misery, cross, and struggle,
With sorrow in the mind, but Jesus in the eye. <note>The "eye" here refers to the "eye of faith" or the "inward eye" of the soul focusing on Christ despite outward suffering.</note>

->JOB 6: 4.<-

---

*—For the arrows of the Almighty are within me, etc.* <note>A reference to the biblical Job, who describes his suffering as being shot by God's arrows. In this mystical context, the "wounding" is seen as a painful but necessary part of spiritual purification and divine love.</note>

<summary>This page presents a poem depicting Jesus as a divine archer who wounds the believer's heart with arrows of love and affliction. It explores the paradox that spiritual suffering leads to purification and ultimate joy, framed by the biblical suffering of Job.</summary>

## XVI.

## *Conversation of the Soul.*

1. **T**his parable <note>referring to the emblem on the previous page depicting Christ shooting arrows at the heart.</note> makes known to us, oh good Lord Jesus, that you also, out of Love, wound the human heart with the arrows of your grace.

2. For since you love the salvation of mankind, oh Lord, you spare no effort toward that end: for what greater grace could you show to your poor creature <note>original: *Schepfel*; a term emphasizing the humble status of the human being as a created work of God.</note> than to wound its heart with the arrows of your Love? For since the heart is, as it were, immovably occupied with love of itself and love of the world, how could it be loosened in any other way?

3. Therefore, oh faithful Savior, finding that my heart is very hard and, as it were, immovably set upon itself and the world: I pray you, that it may please you to show me this great grace, to wound my heart with the arrows of your Love, so that it may once more be ignited in love for you and for your Holy will.

4. Oh good Jesus, at this hour and moment

## *Further Observation*
### *On the XVI. Figure.*
### From J. TAULERUS, <note>Johannes Tauler (c. 1300–1361) was a famous German Dominican mystic. His sermons emphasized the inner life and the "birth of God in the soul."</note>

**O**ne finds very few true lovers now; many want to follow Christ, but only for the sake of the sweetness <note>original: *soetigheid*; a technical term in mysticism for "spiritual consolations" or the pleasant emotional feelings that can accompany prayer.</note>, and if they do not taste that, and are not permitted to be touched with wounded love, then they do not want to follow God. Now, when God sees that he cannot bring or draw those same souls to him to follow him in any other way than with sweetness: then he lures them with **wounded love**, so that he may bring them to **captive love** where they cannot then escape him, and after that they come into **raging love** <note>original: *woedende liefde*; this refers to a divine "frenzy" or an intense, overwhelming spiritual passion that disregards worldly reason.</note>; then they become so strong that they are comforted in all things, and for God’s honor they cast themselves into all mockery and suffering that God wishes to send upon them and rejoice in; and then they offer themselves into the **hidden abyss of the Godhead**, and out of great distress they say:

> O Lord, whether you wish to save us or damn us! That lies within your power; your will be done to us.

And when a person comes to this point, then he has attained that which he was seeking, and it is all the same to God how one arrives there, or in what manner. And so that he may draw every Soul to him, he therefore grants his gift so generously; and serves us with his grace so kindly, solely so that the Soul should offer herself to God in all manner of surrender <note>original: *gelatenheyd*; a central concept in German/Dutch mysticism meaning "releasement" or total yieldedness to God's will.</note>, without any contradiction or excuse.

<summary>This page explains the spiritual progression from seeking emotional "sweetness" to achieving total surrender to God's will. Citing Johannes Tauler, it describes how God uses "wounded love" to lead the soul into an "abyss" of devotion where it accepts both joy and suffering equally.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Tauler, wounded love, captive love, raging love, Godhead, surrender, spiritual sweetness, mystical union, Gelatenheyd</keywords>

->SONG OF SOLOMON 5: 8.<- <note>original: *HOOGL.*; abbreviation for *Hooglied*.</note>

> *I charge you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my Beloved, what will you tell him? That I am sick with love.* <note>The "sickness of love" is a classic mystical theme where the soul feels physically weakened by its intense spiritual longing for union with the Divine.</note>

Spare no arrows, shoot still more;

Pierced and scorched my heart;
Away, flesh and lust of the eyes <note>original: *oogluft*; a reference to 1 John 2:16, representing worldly distractions.</note>, away from here,
Here no sinful fire holds value anymore;
For Heaven’s fire surely consumes
All other fire that is of the world.

Consume, O God, my soul entirely,
Shine through my heart more and more;
Be you, O Jesus! truly my portion,
My screen and shield; my hope and honor.

*Which he had kindled;*
*And* **Jesus** *then became* **King** *in*

## XVII. Figure.
### Here Jesus strengthens the Heart.

O **Jesus**! when your flame of love
First entered into me;
Who could then imagine all that joy?
The **Heart** that swam in a thousand delights;
You kissed me with your mouth, <note>This is a direct reference to Song of Solomon 1:2, "Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth," which in 17th-century mysticism represented the intimate union between the soul and God.</note>
And it made the mournful **Heart** healthy;
You drew my **Heart** through your beams,
Which you let descend into me;
That was the foretaste in those days,
Of eternal love and joy,
Which you come to grant your **Children**,
When you cause your love to be remembered:
O that my **Soul** enjoyed that joy!
And that I might lay down and ever rest
In my **Jesus'** bosom,
Oh! that you kissed me with your mouth:
Descend constantly, O best **Lord**!
Stir up that flame of love once more,
Which lies as if extinguished,
And altogether cast aside.
This is something that the world
Knows not, nor ever rightly sees;
But indeed a **Soul** that was ignited,
And as if drunk from that flame of love;
If **Jesus** gives all this to the **Soul**,
While it lives here on earth,
What sweetness shall he give,
When it shall step out of the mortal part: <note>The "mortal part" refers to the physical body; the poet is reflecting on the greater joys of the afterlife compared to earthly spiritual experiences.</note>
O **Wellspring** full of sweetness!
For this my **Soul** cries out;
Please ignite my deadened **Heart**,
With your flame, for which I plead.

->ISAIAH 33. verse 17.<-

*Your eyes shall see the King in his beauty:*
*they shall see a land that is very far off.*

# XVII.

->*Conversation of the Soul.*<-

<margin>1.</margin> Holy Lord, you have not only wished to wound my Heart with the arrows of your Love: <note>The "arrows of love" is a common mystical motif, suggesting that divine love must "break" or "pierce" the heart to enter it.</note> but seeing that there still lies hidden here and there within it some love for the world and for myself, you have also wished to ignite the same through the flame of your Grace, in such a way that all remaining impure love, lust, and desires, both for myself and for anything that is in the world, might be entirely consumed.

<margin>2.</margin> Do not cease then, O good Jesus, to appear in my Heart: ignite it indeed more and more through your grace into a burning fervor; to fulfill your holy will, and to forsake all selfish and worldly improper love: see, Lord, at this hour I give my heart over to you: ah, appear within it! make it your own dwelling, which otherwise has grown very cold <note>original: *verkout*; literally "chilled." The author uses the metaphor of temperature to describe spiritual apathy.</note> in love. Let me indeed experience the working of your grace within it.

<margin>3.</margin> And you, my Soul, be not now sleepy nor negligent in perceiving <note>original: *waar te nemen*; to observe or take advantage of.</note> this grace:

<note>Key terms from the original Dutch: **Conversation of the Soul** (*Zielen-Gesprek*), **Jesus** (*Jesus*), **Grace** (*Genade*), **Love** (*Liefde*), **Soul** (*Ziele*), **Heart** (*Herte*), **Fervor** (*vurigheyt*), **Dwelling** (*wooninge*).</note>

## XVII. Figure.

Burn me, most beloved Lord,
And let your fire consume me entirely,
Let your Spirit devour my heart,
Just as a spark is devoured
In the hot flame of a great fire;
Let all self-will <note>original: "eygendom"; in mystical literature, this refers to "own-ness" or the ego's attachment to itself rather than material property.</note> now come to shame,
Let love for God be sincere,
No longer for reward like a servant;
My Lord, my God, my Heart’s beloved,
How lovely is your devouring.

See, Jesus stretches out his dear hand,
And sets the whole Heart on fire,
So that the flame it has received,
Must burst out on all sides:
So burn freely, Lord, and according to your demand,
Consume to ash the filthy flesh,
That we, through the fire of love, [made] pure,
And may be entirely translucent. <term>translucent</term> (original: *doorlugtig*; meaning spiritually clear or enlightened, allowing the light of God to pass through the soul).

->SONG OF SOLOMON 8:6, 7.<-

Oh, how my spirit longs so much!

To where it first flowed out from;
Oh! how it sighs for that Lord!

That I, here in flesh and blood,

Thirsting for that eternal good.

Eternal being without end,
Without height, without breadth,
Eternal, Eternal, unmeasured,
Unknown to all spirits:
The flesh is consumed by harsh longing,
The fire of love acts with such power,
Before I am received by You,
And my spirit melts into You.

Along the path prescribed for us,
To learn and live through Christ’s humanity,
Filled full of suffering:

To carve our way through worldly contempt,
That is the right way Home.

## Further Consideration
### On the XVII. Figure. <note>This refers to the seventeenth "emblem" or illustration in the book, a common teaching tool in 17th-century devotional literature.</note>

<margin>2</margin>
<margin>80</margin>

->Love is stronger than Death.<- <note>original: "De Liefde is sterker als de Dood"; a reference to the Song of Solomon 8:6.</note>

That is, through death all things are taken away and separated from man; so also through love all obstacles are separated from the Soul.

*The love of God alienates the soul from the body, and makes her live with God, who is her life.*

When Christ touches the heart of man with His hand,
And sets it in the fire of unmeasured love,
Then it is that the flame of love flares out wide,
And makes man completely forget himself through love.

Ignite my Heart by Your flame,
O Jesus my beloved!
Then that which came from You,
Shall find rest in You again.
Then the Soul, from Your glow
And love in its burning,
Shall fall worshipfully at Your feet,
And make an offering to You;
Then Your love shall consume my soul,
O Jesus, my Dearest Lord.

## On the XVII. Figure.

O most beloved friend of the soul,
My Jesus, my beloved,
How blessed is he who finds You,
Who goes to bind himself to You;
Whose heart You have pierced with Your love
As if with a hot flame.

Consume me through the glow of Your love,
And let Your power from within
Melt away the self <note>original: "’t eygen"; a mystical term referring to the ego or the independent self-will that separates the soul from God.</note> in the mind,
And all the earthly senses:
O Jesus, raised high in worth,
You are my treasure, my shield, my life.

Your love, unfathomable,
Is higher than the stars,
And wider than the earthly realm,
One knows You only from afar;
The abyss of Your goodness
Surpasses all human reason.

Come with Your riches into the heart,
And let my soul live
In You; then the Cross and pain
Of the world shall leave me,
For Your favor and love, O Lord,
Can consume all that is worldly.

<sig>K 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Here</meta>
<vocab>Jesus, Love, Riches, Cross, Heart-Theology, soul, earthly</vocab>

<summary>A devotional poem addressing Jesus as the "friend of the soul," praying for divine love to act as a consuming fire that destroys the ego and earthly attachments. It emphasizes the vastness of God's goodness, which exceeds human understanding and provides a refuge from worldly suffering.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, Jesus, self-denial, mysticism, Heart-Theology, spiritual fire, human reason, transcendence, soul's friend</keywords>

## Figure XVIII.
### Here Jesus Crowns the Heart.

The Way of the Cross <note>original: "Kruys-weg"; refers to the spiritual journey of suffering and imitation of Christ’s passion.</note> has been traveled,
The sweet Jesus, Son of God,
Comes to crown the Heart with palms,
At the end of its pain and struggle.
Now the joyful feast is prepared,
That shall last and exist eternally,
For Jesus, full of Glory,
Desires now to heal the heart completely.
Thus the Soul inherits after pain and Cross,
A joy that can never be described,
For Jesus brings the Soul home,
To live with him forever and always.
Thus the bitter soot <note>original: "roet"; used here metaphorically to represent the dark, grimy residue of worldly suffering or sin, contrasted with the brightness of joy.</note> is transformed
Into gladness, the Cross into rows of joy,
The King Jesus! Highly honored,
Comes himself to lead the soul to Heaven:
Therefore, even if the struggle is somewhat heavy,
The suffering great, here in this life,
It is nothing—yes, less than a hair—
Compared to that which Jesus wants to give us.
And therefore, Christian, step forward,
Courageously through the crosses,
The King Jesus, by his word,
Wants to grant us his joy in exchange.

->2 Cor. 4: 17, 18.<-

> *For our light affliction, which is but for a moment, worketh for us a far more exceeding and eternal weight of glory; while we look not at the things which are seen, but at the things which are not seen. etc.*

<meta>catchword: Here</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the spiritual journey of the heart, depicting Jesus crowning the soul with palms of victory after its trials. It uses the metaphor of the "Way of the Cross" to explain that earthly suffering is insignificant compared to the eternal joy and "weight of glory" promised in the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus, coronation, palms, victory, Way of the Cross, eternal life, 2 Corinthians 4, soul's reward, perseverance, Christian struggle</keywords>

# XVIII.

## Here Jesus Crowns the Heart.

To those who do his will out of love <note>The poem contrasts the fickle, ungrateful nature of "The World" with the steadfast, self-giving nature of Christ, who offers not just rewards, but his own self.</note>.

# XVIII.

## Dialogue of the Soul.

1. In this final example, it is shown to us that it is not in vain to serve you, O faithful <term>Savior</term> <note>original: "Saligmaker", literally 'one who makes blessed'.</note> and Lover of the soul: but that you finally honor and adorn your faithful servants with a glorious Reward and a Crown.

2. Indeed, here it is set before our eyes that you wish to eventually glorify those who love and obey you as a Heavenly King. You wish to grant and place upon them that Crown of Honor which you have won through your bitter suffering and the wearing of a painful Crown of Thorns—just as a King in this world has his Queen crowned.

3. Behold then, O my soul, what your <term>Blood-Bridegroom</term> <note>original: "Bloet-Bruydegom". This term refers to Christ as the mystical spouse of the soul, whose marriage covenant was sealed with his blood.</note> Jesus wishes to grant you; he wants to make a Heavenly Queen out of you; he wants to crown you with infinite Glory in Blessed Eternity.

4. O Soul, see: this shall follow upon your <term>Struggle and Fight</term> <note>original: "Kamp ende Strydt". This refers to the concept of 'spiritual warfare' or the daily moral and spiritual difficulties a believer faces.</note>; an eternal Crown is prepared for you. Oh, so then, fight courageously, and struggle...

<meta>catchword: Ridder (Knight / Rider)</meta>

<summary>This page contains a meditative dialogue reflecting on the soul's ultimate reward, comparing Christ’s spiritual coronation of the believer to a worldly king crowning his queen. It encourages the reader to persevere through spiritual struggles in anticipation of eternal glory.</summary>
<keywords>Soul-Dialogue, Savior, Crown of Honor, Crown of Thorns, Blood-Bridegroom, Eternity, Spiritual Warfare, Reward</keywords>

## Figure XVIII.

The path is finished, the struggle is over,
And Jesus Christ crowns His Bride;
The Holy Heart, which with longing
Has clung to its God alone:
The <term>Seraph</term> <note>original: "Zerafyn", an angelic being associated with the highest order of angels and characterized by fiery love and light.</note>, that beautiful spirit,
Adorns the Bride for the wedding feast,
There shall they hold eternal celebration <note>original: "Hoogtyd", literally "high-time," referring to a solemn festival or a great feast day.</note>,
The joy shall never grow old there;
The Soul that has despised all things <note>Meaning the soul has rejected worldly distractions in favor of spiritual ones.</note>,
Is now completely satisfied with God:
That is something lofty, who can imagine it?
The spirit must melt in such riches.

The Bride of Jesus is crowned
With palms and laurels,
King Jesus shall adorn her
With gold, which shows His beauty:
The radiance outshines the most precious stone,
Now the struggle has fled,
While Jesus leads the invited guests,
His fairest Bride, away to Heaven.

## Figure XVIII.

Praise God, how glorious and how beautiful
Is the Crown of love through grace;
For after so much virtue gained,
And after so much joy enjoyed,
After so many of your signs of love, <note>original: "dyn", an archaic form of "your."</note>
To then still be crowned with salvation: <note>original: "heyl", which carries connotations of both spiritual health and eternal salvation.</note>
Praised be God in eternity,
Who has prepared such things for man.

The world through its false deceit,
Still rewards its servants with ingratitude;
But Jesus! God’s beloved Son,
Gives to the Soul a blessed reward,
A reward, not out of merit, <note>This highlights a central theme in Reformed and Pietist theology: that the believer's "crown" is a gift of God's grace, not something earned by human effort.</note>
But prepared through grace:
The King Jesus, mighty and strong,
Who always crowns His own work; <note>This implies that even the "good works" of a believer are actually the work of Christ within them.</note>
He grants for virtue His blessed reward,
For love His only beloved Son,
He gives Himself and all His goods,
To those who do His will out of love;
Our work, whatever has been done by us
In love, He shall accept;
The Crown of His Glory,
Is also assigned to us by Him.

## S O N G. *To the tune of Petit Bordeaux.* <note>Petit Bordeaux refers to a specific, likely popular, melody of the period to which these lyrics were set for singing.</note>

### 1.

Dearly ransomed Dwellers of Heaven,
Raise now a sweet tone,
Desire now to sing to God’s honor,
Desire now to praise God’s Son;
God’s Son, so high in value,
Promised by the Father,
To His Holy People on Earth,
Who seek Him through much struggle. &c.

### 2.

Desire now to utter psalms of joy,
Eternally to His eternal Praise,
For He now wishes to crown the heart
With Palms; <note>In Christian iconography, the palm branch is a symbol of victory over death and spiritual struggle, often associated with martyrs and those who enter heaven (see Revelation 7:9).</note> He gives you cause
To praise His Holy work,
After so much suffering you have endured,
And to prove His Love,
By thus sanctifying the heart. &c.

### 3.

Joy after suffering causes rejoicing,
Joy after so much struggle and the cross, <note>The "cross" here refers to the personal burdens and spiritual trials of the believer.</note>
Always to rejoice in God,
When one comes home to Heaven;
Here on Earth is no dwelling
That can truly gladden us,
Except with Jesus our King,
Our Bridegroom and Husband. &c.

### 4.

Eternally must the Heavenly citizens
Sing Jesus’ great name,
Eternally above all things,
For He alone was worthy <note>original: "bequaam," meaning fit, capable, or worthy in a theological sense.</note>
To gladden our heart,
Eternally now Hallelujah,
Hallelujah at all times,
Let the Angels follow it. &c.
