->SONG OF SOLOMON 2:16.<- <note>original: "HOOGL. 2:16." abbreviation for *Hooglied*, the "Song of Songs" or "Song of Solomon."</note>

> *My beloved is mine, and I am his.*

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial V with intricate scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>From whence does the loving Soul derive such great boldness, that she allows herself to think that the Divine Majesty should be turned entirely toward her? What is man, that God should even think of him, and cast His eyes upon a child of men? <note>A common theological refrain echoing Psalm 8:4: "What is man, that thou art mindful of him?"</note> And how daring, and how gloriously does this word sound: *my beloved is mine, and I am his.*

God, to whom the whole of Heaven and Earth belongs, without whose permission not even a single leaf from the trees on Earth falls; nor is a small bird forgotten by Him, but He fills all creatures with His divine blessings; has He left all things and forgot-

# Heart-Theology.

L 2

ten, and is he turned to her alone? Is he in love with her alone, with all his heart, senses, and powers? So that she says: *my beloved is mine, and I am his*: or is she loved by him alone? And does he not love his other creatures that he has created? Yes, especially those whom he has bought and redeemed with the precious <note>The Dutch "dieren prys" refers to a "dear" or "costly" price.</note> price of his red blood. And is it not written of him that he is no respecter of persons? <note>A reference to Acts 10:34 or Romans 2:11, meaning God does not show partiality or favoritism based on status.</note> Has he chosen her alone and rejected all other people? Or does she believe that no one is as good, as beautiful, as holy, and virtuous as she? And that she alone loves God with all her heart, that she boldly says: *my beloved is mine, and I am his*. Is she drunk, presumptuous, out of her mind, vain, foolish, or lying, that she speaks such a proud word? She is not haughty, vain, or lying, that is evident; for she loves, and that virtue is very far from all these defects.

But as to whether she is drunk; that I will not excuse; perhaps she has been with her Bridegroom <note>In this tradition, the "Bridegroom" is a common mystical title for Christ, and the "Bride" is the individual soul.</note> and King in the Wine-cellar, and she is filled with new wine that gladdens the heart, just as she confesses in the Book of Love <note>original: "Boek der liefde." A common title for the Song of Solomon/Song of Songs.</note> that at other times the King has led her into his wine-house? <note>A reference to Song of Solomon 2:4: "He brought me to the banqueting house [literally "house of wine"], and his banner over me was love."</note> But it is best to believe that she does not speak that word herself, but the love of which she is full speaks within her; for love has so captured all her senses and powers that she thinks of nothing but him whom she loves.

<summary>This page addresses the soul's "bold" claim of exclusive intimacy with God, explaining that such language is not a sign of pride or exclusion of others, but a state of spiritual ecstasy—a "drunkenness" of love inspired by the Song of Solomon.</summary>
<keywords>Heart-Theology, Song of Solomon, spiritual intoxication, Bridegroom of the Soul, redemption, divine love, mystical union</keywords>

<header>->Lively<-</header>

For she knows for certain that her beloved is no less hers than she is his, and she has not only emptied her heart of all other things, but she has also passed over and forgotten all harmful distractions <note>The Dutch "menigvuldigheden" (multiplicities) refers to the many worldly thoughts and cares that distract the soul from the "One" God.</note>, and she has so embraced that one Divine One (which is necessary above all things) <note>A reference to the "one thing needful" from Luke 10:42.</note> with love, and set him before the eyes of her heart, that she thinks, imagines, dreams, seeks, thirsts, or desires nothing else—yes, she desires to see no other object than him.

And she hopes and firmly trusts that her beloved loves her as uniquely, heartily, burningly, and faithfully as she does him: for she has often tasted this through certain experience. For in every turning, when she turns to him with all her heart, she finds that he is much sweeter, friendlier, more powerful, more comforting, and lovelier to her than she can bear or desire: yes, even if her beloved had no more creatures in Heaven or on Earth to care for, and had fallen upon her with love alone with all that he is, he could not be more to her, and she could not desire more from him.

And therefore she says well: *My beloved is mine, and I am his*; <note>Song of Solomon 2:16. This verse is central to the "mystical marriage" between the soul and God.</note> she speaks this not in vain, but out of full knowledge, faith, and love: thus her heart speaks this good, lovely word, and she says very quickly and skillfully: *My beloved is mine, and I am his*. Enclosing a great, broad meaning in few words; for God is so un-

comprehensible and immeasurable, that he through his Divine might: fills Heaven and Earth as [if for] one man, and he is to all people who love him, as much as if, with all that he was capable of, he loved only one person: for he is not scattered, distracted, troubled, or anxious among many people, nor is he cramped, burdened, or bound to one person. and given. And as Paul says, he who has given us his only Son, has he not given us all things with him? <note>original: "die ons syn eenigen Soone gegeven heeft, en heeft hy ons met hem niet alle dingen gegeven?" — A reference to Romans 8:32.</note> And as a loving soul being full of faith, that Word of God lives: the Son of God is born and given to us alone <note>This echoes Isaiah 9:6: "For unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given."</note>, so she does not doubt that he is given to her; for she believes, and she loves, therefore she says: *My beloved is mine.* <note>Song of Solomon 2:16. The text follows the mystical tradition of "Brautmystik" (Bride-mysticism), where the individual soul is the bride and Christ is the Bridegroom.</note>

But she acknowledges that so precious a gift as God himself is not given because of any merits she had earned; but only out of grace and love. Thus this love demands that she must respond to this love with all her power, and that she must give herself entirely to the one who has given himself entirely to her, just as Christ was not born for himself, nor lived for himself, but for the believers who wish to be submissive to him and love him; so a faithful, loving soul acknowledges that if she is to respond to and satisfy the love of her <term>Savior<gloss>Saligmaker: literally "maker of blessedness/salvation"</gloss></term> and Bridegroom with love, then she may not be born or live for herself, but for Christ her beloved; and therefore she says: *My beloved is mine, and I am his.*

She does not say this to boast that she has ever [fully] reciprocated the love of her beloved, or loved him as much as he has loved her; but she says: *my beloved is mine, and I am his;* as if she were saying: I acknowledge what my beloved is to me and what he has done for me, and I desire to do the same for him in return; this demands

# EMBLEM,
## *On a Soul that wished to give half the Heart to Jesus.*

Not so, unwise Soul, not so,
    It is a shame that one offers such a Lord
Half the Heart in hypocrisy;
    You must learn a more faithful love.
If you wish to be the Bride of Jesus,
    If you wish to court the most beautiful Bridegroom,
Be purely rid of all "I" and "mine,"
    Purely rid of all <term>adulteries<gloss>Hoereryen: In mystical literature, this refers to "spiritual adultery," or the soul being unfaithful to God by loving worldly things.</gloss></term>.
He alone must be beloved,
    Or do you not know his high worth?
How he is God’s only Child,
    A Lord of Heaven and of Earth:
A source of all good and sweet,
    Of power, of light, of joy and life,
Who creates all, and feeds all,
    And has given all beauty.

# ASPIRATIONS
<note>original: "Versuchtingen." In this devotional context, it refers to the "sighing" or "breathing out" of the soul toward God—ardent spiritual longings or aspirations.</note>
### AND
## Spiritual Outgoings of the Soul
### To her Bridegroom
# JESUS CHRIST.

Who dares, my good God, of your love
->to boast;<-
Who dares call themselves the guardian or seeker of your love,
Who, through the love of your love—that comforting pain—
Does not a thousand times wish to be killed for you!
It is no true love, where earthly <term>understandings<gloss>verſtanden: Referring here to the rational, self-interested mind as opposed to the surrendered heart.</gloss></term>,
Carried away by self-interest, pawn their love to yours:
He who looks only to the sufficiency of the present,
Finds neither true love, nor eternal loving.
How may you, insignificant creature—who at first was but empty earth,
Born to true vanity and a nothingness of being—
Exalt your status, as if you, in your very nature,
In being, duty, or love, were worth anything more than nothing?
->worth?<-
Oh, what sadness! Who has ever understood this strange game,
That love, like an <note>The "executioner of love" is a common mystical paradox; the idea that divine love "kills" the ego or the worldly self through a "sweet" suffering in order to bring the soul to higher life.</note>executioner, with sweet pinches of affection,
With internal sighing, and with external pain,
Gives food to love, so that love may be sweet?
Here, then, the breath of life is found in such a state,
Even though the lover swims in deadly wounds:

Where true <term>zeal<gloss>yver: Intense spiritual fervor or dedication.</gloss></term> is kindled around the heart,

Where Heaven triumphs as Earth is lost;
Where poverty gives riches, where shame gives birth to glory:
Where against current and wind, the ship sails all the better.

For without your favor, no labor can benefit me:

# Heart-Theology.
I i.
<meta>catchword: By</meta>

Your presence is my all, your absence turns to naught,
Even that which seems best, if without you it is wrought.
Grant me then through grace that presence; please convert
What was twisted within me; and in an orderly way teach
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What I am in essence, and what your *Godhead* is;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So that I, thus taught, may in no way go astray.
Enlighten my darkness, awaken the dull senses;
Awaken the sleepy heart, so that I may begin,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The entrance to the <term>cross<gloss>kruys: In this mystical context, the cross represents both the historical sacrifice of Christ and the spiritual path of self-denial and suffering the believer must undergo.</gloss></term>, and weigh with understanding,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why this wood, and fruit, was planted on the earth.
So that I, in that fruit, and in its painful dying,
May be nourished, and here in death learn to inherit life.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So that I seek nowhere else glory, path, or house,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nor food, nor drink, nor comfort, nor strength, but in the cross.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So that I may see my light stripped of luster, in shame,
My Enlightener at the <term>press<gloss>pars: A common mystical metaphor referring to the "Winepress of the Wrath of God" or the "Winepress of Love," where Christ was crushed like a grape to produce the wine of salvation.</gloss></term>, shackled in bonds;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What <note>original: "worm." A reference to Psalm 22:6, "But I am a worm and not a man," traditionally interpreted as a prophecy of Christ's humiliation.</note>worm was ever crushed like my true *God*?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What man was ever oppressed by such a rejected fate?
Alas! On the contrary, I, true fuel for sins,
Am found only in lust and in luxury;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;My pride rears up, as if I were like *God*,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I who in my being am nothing more than <term>mire<gloss>flyk: Mud or filth; a common term used in 17th-century devotional literature to emphasize human lowliness compared to divine purity.</gloss></term>.
The *Creator* is mocked, the *creature* lets himself be praised,
And demands that men shall do him homage and show highest honor;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The *Lord* drinks myrrh and gall, the servant swims in wine.
He who is born a slave wants to be the highest ruler.

### Living

<note>original: "Levendige" — likely referring to a "Living Faith" or "Living Water," a common thematic header in Pietist literature of this era.</note>

> By that which so displeases you! Grant that I may at last turn
> My eye away from vanity, that supreme defect.
> Everything I have constantly and earnestly sought to investigate,
> Whether among talkative crowds or in learned books,
> I find no true meaning, no other right account,
> Except that the truth, O Lord, lies buried within you.
> Is everything outside of you then nothing but a lie?
> Is everyone left ashamed, and without you, deceived?
> Who should wonder then, that through the shock of deceit
> We find nothing but what causes us <term>deadly harm<gloss>dootlyk let: injury that leads to spiritual death.</gloss></term>?
> We desire nothing but self-imposed bonds;
> We encounter nothing but unknown shames,
> Born out of <term>sin<gloss>sonde: In this context, the inherent state of separation from the Divine.</gloss></term>, swaddled in guilt,
> Worshipping our own downfall, because it is gilded.
> Who finds themselves so confined in the filthiest of hovels?
> Who is so locked in iron chains,
> As those who, carried away by the wicked deceit of <term>vanity<gloss>ydelheyt: The emptiness and futility of worldly pursuits compared to eternal truth.</gloss></term>,
> Seek nothing but their own will under the guise of "loose freedom"?
> Driven constantly by a foolish discipline
> In speech, in sight, in hearing, in an unrestrained life,
> In the consumption of food and drink, in work or in rest,
> Aiming only for ultimate enjoyment—the fosterer of lust.
> O troublesome danger, tied up in golden snares!
> When they are revealed to my eyes, they terrify my soul so:
> No, Heaven—send instead a small ray of your light
> To clearly open my <term>weakened eyes<gloss>leepende geſicht: Literally "bleary-eyed vision," implying spiritual sight that is dimmed or watering due to the "smoke" of worldly vanity.</gloss></term>.
> Come, Loving Masters, convert my heart and senses;
> Come, <term>Masterly Love<gloss>meeſterlyke liefde: A divine, authoritative love that acts as a teacher or lord over the soul.</gloss></term>, dwell within my soul—come in!
> And even if your presence were a fountain of pain,
> I desire, if you so desire, to be cherished even in that way.

# Heart Theology.

### III.

Let the song sound a bit higher,
Let the pen explain a bit more clearly,
Of the sweet <term>Heavenly bread<gloss>Hemels brood: A reference to Manna or Christ as the "Bread of Life," signifying spiritual nourishment that sustains the soul.</gloss></term>,
That one finds in pain and distress.
It seems like absurd laws
That place death alongside life,
Pressing the untasted sweetness
Out of the <term>myrrh<gloss>myrh: An aromatic resin used in incense and embalming; in mystical poetry, it symbolizes the "bitterness" of suffering that leads to spiritual purification.</gloss></term> and bitter soot.
Yet even so, my Lord of Lords,
You seek to teach with your word,
That the pains of this <note>original: "dal" — referring to the "valley of tears" (vallis lacrimarum), a common metaphor for earthly life.</note> valley,
That sighing from sad misfortune,
That the cruelty of the executioners,
Who conspire with death and the abyss,
That the evil that hovers around us
Gives the true material for glory?
They are but brief moments,
If we arrange love alongside suffering,
Where the <term>God-learned soul<gloss>Godsgeleerde Ziel: Not an academic theologian, but a "theodidaktos"—a soul taught directly by God through experience rather than books.</gloss></term>
Fell into pleasure and pain together.
Where God’s beloved <note>original: "swalen" — swallows. In 17th-century emblem books, the swallow often represented the soul or the believer seeking a home in God's altars (referencing Psalm 84).</note> swallows
Descend into the heart of love;
Ah, what is not strived for there,
That the world so despises!
Come then a little closer to me, Lord,
And ordain, my good Father,
In my life, in death,
In my abundance, or need,
In my youth, or gray days,

# Vibrant

<meta>catchword: Yes</meta>

In my intermittent bouts,
In the darkness, or by day,
That I may always suffer.
Burn my <term>coarse limbs<gloss>onbeschofte leden: In 17th-century Dutch, "onbeschoft" referred to something unrefined, raw, or unpolished; here, it describes the physical body that has not yet been disciplined by divine grace.</gloss></term>,
Let me be trodden underfoot,

Steadily turned against me.

Let me enjoy neither refuge nor comfort,
Which the earth, or the earthly day,
Is accustomed to give to man.

# Theology of the Heart

Indeed, if anyone should think
That suffering was to be found
Near Divine beauty,
Where nothing but glory dwells.
Nevertheless, I would with longing—
Even where pains surround me,

Since my Creator, in his struggle,
With the <note>original: "sleutel van het lyden" — the key of suffering. A common metaphor in mystical literature suggesting that the only way to "unlock" the gates of heaven or deep spiritual intimacy is through the experience of pain.</note> key of suffering

Where one enjoys God eternally.

->IV<-

Human <term>nature<gloss>Aard: Here referring to mankind or the human condition, specifically those possessing "reason and understanding."</gloss></term> with reason and understanding,
Take now this teaching to heart,
That suffering, that sorrow,
Does not look at all upon <note>original: "geen verdiensten" — no merits. This reflects a central Protestant/Pietist tenet that suffering is not a way to "earn" salvation through one's own works, but rather a way to be shaped by God.</note> merits.
Indeed, as far as I do not stray,
In this suffering is the school
Where one learns to walk the paths
That my Creator has trod.
He who came down from Heaven,
And took us as his pupil,
And in the right manner,
Pointed out the ways to Paradise,
Out of his abundance of love,
With the shedding of his blood.
What amazement, what great terror,
Did he suffer every moment!
How beaten, how mocked,

<meta>catchword: How</meta>

<summary>This page describes suffering not as a punishment or a means to earn salvation, but as a "school" and a "key" that allows the believer to follow the path carved by Christ. It emphasizes that even in the presence of Divine glory, the experience of affliction is a "great happiness" because it mirrors the Creator's own journey.</summary>

### Living

How reviled as a fool,
How rolled in his blood,
How pierced through hand and foot,
How refreshed with bitter <term>gall<gloss>gal: A reference to the biblical account of the Crucifixion (Matthew 27:34), where Jesus was offered wine mixed with bitter herbs. In spiritual literature, it symbolizes the peak of earthly rejection and bitterness.</gloss></term>,

To endure the same fate
With your Lord and God?
Let the day be happy

And even if the necessity of suffering
Oppressed me unto death;
Dying I know for certain,
That <note>original: "soo sterven, leven is" — dying so, is living. This paradox is central to the "Theology of the Heart," suggesting that the death of the ego and the physical body leads to true spiritual vitality.</note> dying so is living:
For the death that Christ suffers
Is the death that gladdens all.
It is the life of my heart
Through which the Soul becomes living.

My Lord, it is now time to press even higher

Do not strip me of the desire to suffer, as my friend;
And though I were swallowed up by pain in sorrow,
Yet grant that I remain with that which the pain deserves.

# Theology of the Heart.

### V I I.

I was recently quite greatly dismayed,
Because I had paid too much attention
To the earth, and to earthly goods,
Which causes man to be alienated from God;
Which so ravishes the heart’s thoughts,
Which presses down high spiritual senses,
Which praises the world above Heaven,
Which shows nothing but <note>original: "kromme wegen" — crooked ways. This imagery contrasts with the "straight path" of the righteous often cited in Psalms and Proverbs.</note> crooked ways;
Which wishes and wants for nothing but the temporal,
Which is so far from the eternal shield;
Which curses suffering as its enemy,
Which counts nothing but <gloss>soete dagen: sweet days; a life of comfort and ease devoid of spiritual struggle.</gloss> sweet days;
Which easily loses God's love,
And in its place chooses its own <note>original: "eygen kieft" — chooses its own [self-will]. This refers to the concept of 'Eigenwille' or self-will, which mystical tradition views as the primary obstacle to divine union.</note>.
All this, in the quiet night,
Carried me to this thought:
How is it my love, my holy lot,
How is it my love, my great God,
How am I so twisted in mind,
That I love not You, but myself?

# Heart-Theology.

Unworthy without reward,
Before your Divine throne?
To despise you, to provoke you to anger,
And yet to burn with the flame of your love?
  I will not, or cannot understand,
  How both can go together.
Constantly setting your loving laws,
Aside from earnestness and duty,
And yet considering it a great pain
To not be loved by you?
Now pursuing matters, then choosing,
Now searching, then losing,
  World, Heaven, Water, Fire;
  Quiet customs, great clamor?
Can one indeed these great differences,
Wish for together, will together?
  Can such a struggle exist
  In the heart at the same time?
Let my soul indeed confess this,
And weep with constant tears,
  That through unbridled power, <note>original: "onbesnoeyde macht" — literally "unpruned power," referring to the wild, undisciplined impulses of the human spirit that have not been "trimmed" by divine grace.</note>
  Many strive against reason.
Push aside destruction full of pains,
For I see the dawn <note>original: "daag'raat" — the dawning of spiritual insight or divine truth that exposes the heart's internal contradictions.</note> shining,
  Which so clarifies my eyes,
  That the deceit reveals itself.
I can now surely feel,
That this contradictory churning,
  That this love, and the hatred,
  That this always good and evil:

### X.

Though I am a vessel full of sins,
A spring of the most wicked poison,
Grown old in the impulse of evil,
A being full of desired wounds,
That does nothing but hiss with foul stench;
Yet I dare, <term>Creator</term> <note>original: "Schepper" — referring to God as the primary architect of existence.</note>, to come seeking
<term>Reconciliation</term> <note>original: "Versoening" — the act of making amends or being brought back into harmony with God.</note> for my crime,
Healing for my greatest evil:
And do not wish to curse your creature;
Look, Lord, upon yourself, and my state.
What am I, uncreated Being?
A vanity, a mere nothing,
Much more fragile than the weakest reed,
Which, having risen out of death,
Flies back again to earth and dying.
And how shall the creature name you?
You who are all my joy and happiness,
Who in your <term>Godhead</term> <note>original: "Godheyd" — the essential nature or essence of God, often described as unchanging and beyond human suffering.</note> suffer nothing,
A wisdom that the Heavens praise,
To have existed before all time.
Well then, a little worm of the earth,
But you a grasper of the globe, <note>original: "vatter van den kloot" — "Kloot" (sphere/globe) refers to the entire world or cosmos. This imagery portrays God holding the entire universe in His hand.</note>
Who encloses it all in your bosom,
Who out of your wide power gave birth
To that which has received the name of "great."
Immeasurable then are the boundaries
Of your widespread dominion,

If your <term>mercy</term> <note>original: "barmhertigheyd" — a central concept in Dutch Pietism, emphasizing God's compassion for the miserable.</note> is there as well,

If you were to set me aside?

That over my <term>misdeed</term> <note>original: "misdaat" — a transgression or sin.</note> your righteous <term>wrath</term> <note>original: "gramschap" — divine anger or indignation against sin.</note> broke.
Grant this ignited fire to climb in its circle,
So that your fire of love might find itself with mine,
And let <term>penitent</term> <note>original: "boetvaardig" — characterized by a state of humble repentance and a desire for atonement.</note> moisture descend from on high,
So that I might be bedewed, washed, and dried.

## XII.

When also pale death with arrows of remorse,
Pressed out of the body the powers of the soul:
Yet I hold firm, my God, that <term>trust</term> <note>original: "betrouwen" — the soul's reliance on God's faithfulness; a core virtue in 17th-century Dutch Reformed piety.</note> in you
Shall make me live, though I fell into a faint.
The chains that so terribly surround my spirit,
And with their heavy weight press me into the ground;
Yes, through the great danger, force me as if to despair;
Those also serve for comfort, and make me sound.
But not out of my own strength, but through the sweet breezes,
Which I find in the constant duty of your <term>worship</term> <note>original: "aanbidding" — the act of adoration or prayerful devotion.</note>:
Happy then the soul, which takes pleasure in the pain

<summary>This page contains a poetic prayer for spiritual transformation, asking God to replace an "icy heart" with a fire of devotion, and concludes with a meditation on finding strength in God even when faced with the "chains" of despair and the approach of death.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, divine love, wrath, worship, spiritual trial, death, heart-theology, trust</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic meditation from page 187. The previous section concluded with the soul finding comfort even in the "heavy weight" of despair. Here, the author describes how the physical and mental infirmities of the human condition are dissolved by the "Sun of God" when the soul chooses the path of love and suffering.</meta>

Necessarily receives, while she loves you.
The mists of understanding, the dullness of the senses
The sickness of the flesh, the shortness of the breath:
Vanish before the Sun of God, for those who love him,
Providing for him food, and quenching of the thirst.
I will then never more think of other means,
For stillness of mind, and comforting rest:
But with love travel straight through the way of suffering,
And come to possession of the <term>celestial coast</term> <note>original: "overaartſche kuſt" — literally 'over-earthly,' referring to the shores of heaven or the eternal afterlife.</note>.

---

->XIII.<-

Love which (so it seemed) could not manage alone,
Would choose a <term>companion</term> <note>original: "geſel" (gezel) — a partner, associate, or companion.</note>, and make them happy,
And who, out of simple favor, [she would] enrich with her goods,
Honor with her beauty, and <term>cleanse</term> <note>original: "verſchoonen" — in this context, it carries the dual meaning of making beautiful and washing clean/excusing.</note> with her blood.
This was proclaimed as something new in large and small alleys;
And see, to such service everyone is well inclined:
Thus a <term>crowd</term> <note>original: "rot" — a 17th-century term for a troop or gathering of people.</note> gathers of high and low <term>folk</term> <note>original: "liên" (lieden) — people or common folk.</note>,
Who undertake to look upon *Love* from her throne.
One of that great crowd (I dare not think ill of it)
Who was called *Suffering*, has *Love* without feigning
From the midst of the heap, that adorned multitude,
Chosen as companion, seated beside her.
And see, this rare pair were so bound together,
That *Suffering* without *Love* is no longer found,

<meta>The catchword at the bottom of the page is "Noch" (Yet), indicating the sentence continues on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a meditation on the soul's journey to the "celestial coast" through suffering and introduces an allegory where Love chooses Suffering as her inseparable companion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic sequence from page 188, where Love has just chosen Suffering as her inseparable companion from a large crowd of suitors. The poem now explores the paradoxical necessity of pain within the experience of true love and the natural world.</meta>

# Heart-Theology.
<meta>catchword: Un-</meta>

Nor Love without Pain; who would have ever thought,
That Love without Pain, would not be esteemed as Love?
This <term>Princely Maiden</term> <note>A personification of Love, depicted here as royal and pure.</note> then joins herself to suffering,
In displeasure and in pleasure, in peace and in strife:
In such a tight bond, as never was friend with friend,
No brother with his brother, no mother with her child.
This love calls her joy and sadness both together,
One from a friend's well-being, the other from his misfortune.
He who is a lover in spirit, withdraws the bread from his own mouth,
And gives it to the one he loves to alleviate his need.
If heaven would once permit this love within your heart;
It is certain that this sweet would bring its bitter with it:
For this is love's pain, and the most difficult <term>struggle</term> <note>original: "ſtryd" (strijd) — meaning conflict, battle, or spiritual wrestling.</note>,
If love finds no one for whom she suffers sorrow.
Also in a perfected will, these are the very customs:
That joy has no taste unless there is suffering;
That in the righteous man, the highest state
Also receives greater pleasure through <term>humble torment</term> <note>original: "nederig gequel" — refers to the spiritual discipline of enduring earthly trials with humility.</note>.
Nature turns earth into gold and precious stones,
The foam into mother-of-pearl, ivory from dry bones:
And whatever ever appears so precious to human eyes,
Has its origin in struggle, and without it, disappears.
Where does one find such struggle as between air and earth,
As between fire and moisture? Yet this struggling brought forth
This precious growth; thus I hold it as certain,
That nowhere does good arise except where there has been struggle.

<summary>This page presents a poetic meditation on the inseparable nature of love and suffering, arguing that true joy and all precious things in nature—like gold or pearls—only arise through conflict and trial.</summary>
<keywords>Love, Pain, Suffering, Spiritual Struggle, Nature, Paradox of Joy, Heart-Theology, Self-sacrifice</keywords>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic sequence from page 189, concluding the argument that spiritual growth and true love require the "bitter" experience of struggle and the "mortification" of worldly desires.</meta>

# Living
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

Do not take grief then, though evil inclinations
Urge you toward sin, and battle you daily:
Make a virtue of necessity; <term>mortification</term> <note>original: "versterving" — the spiritual practice of "dying" to one's worldly desires and physical impulses to focus on the soul.</note> wins the right,
Reflect that our life is nothing other than a battle.
Let nothing else please you then, calm Soul,
Than to join constant suffering to your steady labor:
In works, in thoughts, in speech, and in silence,
In the earnest pursuit of that which you do not even want.
In the needless anxiety of providing for everything,
That is useful today, and that one strives for tomorrow:
And if <term>carefulness</term> <note>original: "sorgvuldigheyd" — here meaning an anxious solicitude or worldly worry about the future.</note> has slipped away for a time,
Then take up that same diligence once again,
Desiring with industry to desire no things,
Lest love's tender duty be harmed in the slightest:
To love nothing but the Lord, from whom love descends,
And without whose love, our love finds no love at all.
Here is the supreme good; these are the precious treasures,
Which, without the ray of love, no human heart can grasp;
It is the very core that brings forth eternal life:
Here is the right path, in which one must die.
Grant us then, Giver of love, to always enjoy
The truth of this love, for the sake of which we earlier abandoned
The discipline of <term>self-love</term> <note>original: "eygen liefd'" — the selfish ego or attachment to one's own will, which must be surrendered to receive God's grace.</note>; Lord, accept this prayer,
So that we may stand in your grace eternally.

<summary>This page concludes a poetic reflection on the necessity of spiritual struggle, framing life as a constant battle where suffering and the denial of self-love lead to the "supreme good" of divine love.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual warfare, mortification, divine love, self-denial, grace, prayer, devotion</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>The text continues the spiritual themes of the previous pages, focusing on the inner struggle between worldly inclinations and the pursuit of divine love. The page number 190 suggests it is part of a larger collection of meditations or hymns.</meta>

### Levendige
<note>Likely part of a title such as "Living Water" or "Living Faith," common in 17th-century Dutch devotionals.</note>

Do not be grieved, though evil inclinations
Urge you toward sin and battle you daily:
Make a virtue of necessity; mortification <note>The practice of self-denial or "dying to the self" to overcome worldly desires.</note> wins the right,
Remember that our life is nothing but a struggle.
Therefore, calm Soul, let nothing else satisfy you,
Than to join constant suffering to your steady labor:
In work, in meditation, in speech, and in silence,
In the earnest seeking of that which you do not even desire.
In the needless anxiety of providing for all things
That are useful today, or sought for tomorrow:
And if this care should vanish for a time,
Then take up that care once more with diligence;
Desiring also with industry to desire nothing
That could in the least harm love’s tender duty:
To love nothing but the Lord, from whom love descends,
And without whose love, our love can find no love;
Here is the supreme good, these are the precious treasures,
Which, without the beam of love, no human heart can grasp;
It is the very core that brings eternal life:
Here is the true path, in which one must die.
Grant then, Giver of Love, that we may always enjoy
The truth of this love, for which we once abandoned
The discipline of self-love <note>original: "eygen liefd"; refers to egoism or the selfish love of one's own will.</note>; Lord, accept this prayer,
So that we may stand in Your grace for eternity.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page contains a devotional poem urging the soul to embrace spiritual struggle and self-mortification as the only path to experiencing true divine love. It concludes with a prayer for God's grace and the strength to abandon selfish desires.</summary>
